# STATUS — Comvivo build tracker

**Current milestone:** M1 — Storage & domain core (in progress, started 2026-11-03)  
**Last updated:** 2026-11-05  
**Last green gate:** **M0, 2026-11-03** — `main` green on `e2ebfb9` across all eleven workflows; 1117 tests, 97.33% statements, CI 75 s, 23/23 tasks ([report](#milestone-report))  
**Plan:** [`14-milestones.md`](14-milestones.md) is authoritative for task IDs and order.  
**Operating mode:** autonomous (`CLAUDE.md` §5). Ask the owner only for money, credentials/account actions, or a 2-minute real-chat smoke test.  
**Repo:** https://github.com/faizannraza/comvivo (renamed from `village` on 2026-11-01 when D-2 landed; see DL-8)

## How to update this file

- **Start of a session:** read this file first. Set *Current milestone* and *Last updated* (YYYY-MM-DD).
- **Before a milestone:** fill in its *Execution note*: task order (normally the table order in `14`), which `‖` tasks go to sub-agents, spike plan, risks, and what you need from the human (credentials, hardware, money, publishing approvals). Check `14` §0.6 for owner decisions and §0.7 for open cross-doc issues.
- **Per task:** tick `[x]` only after the AC are met and `pnpm verify` is green. Append the commit hash, e.g. `- [x] M3-T5 RichText AST & parser — a1b2c3d`. Log any deviation under *Plan changes* in the same commit.
- **Spikes:** set the status (`open` → `running` → `passed` / `failed → fallback`), the decision and an evidence link.
- **Owner decisions (autonomous mode since 2026-11-01, `CLAUDE.md` §5):** the owner delegated D-1…D-8, spine changes and approvals to Claude Code, except the **money** parts (D-4 budget, paid services, domains, paid reviews), which must be asked once and recorded. At each checkpoint in `14` §0.6, decide with critical thinking and the `15` §1 recommendation. Record the answer, the rationale, the date and "Claude Code (delegated)" in the table below, and add an ADR when the decision is architectural.
- **End of a milestone:** paste the exit-gate results with numbers into *Milestone report*, list cut-lines used and known gaps, update *Last green gate*, have an independent reviewer sub-agent audit the milestone, merge it, and continue to the next milestone (no stop; `CLAUDE.md` §1.4).
- **Deferring something you found:** if a task uncovers a real defect you are not fixing now, put it under *Deferred follow-ups* with the reason for deferring and the task that should close it. A follow-up recorded only inside a *Plan changes* cell is findable but not actionable.
- **Never** delete history. Strike through superseded text with `~~…~~` and add a dated line.

### H-1 — proposed API budget

Anthropic list prices used for the estimates (cached 2026-10-06 in `11` §8; re-verified at `M8-T22`): Opus 5.5 $4/$20 per MTok, Sonnet 5.5 $2/$10, Haiku 5.5 $0.10/$0.50, Batches −50%.

| Bucket | What it buys | When | Cap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Development smoke + spikes | `SP-4` (per-message effort + cache behaviour, refusal-fallback beta), `SP-5`/E-CAT tool-catalog experiment, per-milestone live smoke, `pnpm evals:live` spot checks | M2–M15 | **$100** |
| Gate-triggered live evals (reduced profile) | Discretion / authority / injection red-team suites plus judges on real models, run **at each milestone gate** rather than nightly | M5–M13 | **$250** |
| Full hardening run | The ≥ 500-scenario red-team at full size across Opus 5.5 / Sonnet 5.5 / Haiku 5.5 with an OpenAI cross-check; extraction F1 on the labelled multilingual corpus | M13 | **$150** |
| Release gates | `quality-gates.yml` for 0.1 alpha, 0.5 beta, 1.0, plus one launch rehearsal | M8, M12, M15 | **$100** |
| | | **Total** | **$600** |

- **Hard daily cap $40**, enforced in the eval runner: a run that would exceed the remaining budget refuses to start rather than truncating.
- **Why not the plan's nightly profile.** `11` §8 prices the full nightly profile at ≈ $850/month and the reduced profile at ≈ $370/month. Both assume a human team working over calendar months. Running continuously, the same coverage comes from gate-triggered runs at a fraction of the cost, because the deterministic adversarial fake gateways carry the per-PR guarantee by design (`15` D-4 interim behaviour). If the owner prefers a true nightly cadence, that is ≈ $370/month for the reduced profile.
- **Open-source credits.** I will verify the current Anthropic programme and hand over a prepared application; applying costs nothing. If credits are granted we upgrade to the full nightly profile at no cash cost.
- Spend is recorded in *API spend ledger* below after every live run.

### H-9 — Cloudflare Pages for the documentation site

The docs site builds and its links validate, but nothing is published yet. `13` §3.C.2 chose
Cloudflare Pages over GitHub Pages because GitHub Pages has a 100 GB/month soft bandwidth limit;
with direct upload the free plan's 500 builds/month is never touched, because the build happens in
our own CI.

Two workflows are already written and SHA-pinned, and both no-op with a clear step summary until
these exist — rather than failing every pull request with a credentials error, which would train
everybody to ignore a red check.

1. **Create a free Cloudflare account** and a **Pages project** by direct upload (no Git
   integration — `docs.yml` builds, and `docs-preview.yml`/`docs-deploy.yml` upload).
2. **Create an API token** scoped to *Cloudflare Pages: Edit* on that account only. Nothing else.
3. Add to this repository:
   - secret `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN`
   - secret `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID`
   - variable `DOCS_CLOUDFLARE_PROJECT` — the Pages project name
4. Point the apex domain at it once `comvivo.dev` exists (H-7).

Until then the site is reviewable with `pnpm docs:dev`, which shows all 104 pages including the
drafts a production build excludes.

### H-10 — a Raspberry Pi 5 afternoon (M1 spikes)

**Why it matters.** `00` §3.7 promises Comvivo runs on a Raspberry Pi, and three M1 decisions turn
on whether it actually does: whether the encrypted-SQLite stack loads at all on arm64 Linux
(**SP-1**), whether the backup cipher is fast enough to be used by default rather than behind a
flag (**SP-2**, threshold 40 MB/s), and whether the `02` §3.16.4 query budgets hold on the slowest
machine we claim to support (`M1-T33`). Getting those wrong is expensive later: the first is a
dependency choice, the second is a default, the third is a published promise.

**What I need.** A Pi 5 with 8 GB and a 64-bit OS, reachable over SSH or with a keyboard, for about
an afternoon. No Comvivo account, no credentials, no spend.

**What you do.** When `M1-T2` lands I will write `scripts/bench/pi.sh`, so the whole session is:

```sh
git clone https://github.com/faizannraza/comvivo && cd comvivo
./scripts/bench/pi.sh          # installs nothing outside the clone; prints a report
```

Then paste the report back. It prints a markdown table I drop into
`docs/architecture/storage-spike.md` and `storage-benchmarks.md` verbatim, with the hardware and OS
version it detected, so the provenance of every number is in the file.

**If you have no Pi.** Nothing stops. The substitute, stated in `STATUS.md` and in the docs rather
than glossed: every row is measured on **darwin-arm64** (the build machine) and on **linux-x64**
(GitHub Actions), and the arm64-Linux column says `not measured — needs H-10` instead of carrying a
number. Where a threshold decides a default — SP-2's 40 MB/s — I take the **conservative** branch
and document that the measurement which would allow the faster one has not been made. An
arm64-Linux *build* smoke test runs in CI under QEMU, which proves the modules load and compile but
says nothing useful about throughput, and will be labelled that way.

### H-6 — secure the Comvivo assets

D-2 is decided (**`Comvivo`**, [ADR-024](../adr/ADR-024-product-name.md)) and the repository is already public, so the
window between the decision and securing these names is the only squatting risk in the project. All were verified free
on 2026-11-01. In priority order:

1. **GitHub organisation `comvivo`** — https://github.com/organizations/plan → Free. This also claims
   **`ghcr.io/comvivo`**, the container namespace the release pipeline publishes to. Afterwards, transfer
   `faizannraza/comvivo` into the org (Settings → Danger Zone → Transfer); GitHub keeps redirects, and `BRAND.repo`
   is the single line that changes.
2. **npm organisation `comvivo`** — needs `npm login`, then `npm org create comvivo` (free for public packages).
   This claims the `@comvivo` scope. Also claim the unscoped `comvivo` and `create-comvivo`, and defensively
   `convivo` and `create-convivo` as deprecation stubs, because English-first speakers will reach for `con-`.
   All five are currently free. Do **not** publish placeholder packages — npm discourages squatting and
   `13` §3.A.4 says real alpha builds ship at M8.
3. **Domain `comvivo.dev`** (~$12/year; this is the money part of H-7). Turn on registrar lock, DNSSEC and 2FA.
   `.org`, `.sh`, `.ai` and `.app` are also free if you want them cheaply. Until a domain exists, `BRAND.urls`
   point at GitHub Pages.
4. **Handles**: X `@comvivo`, Mastodon `@comvivo@fosstodon.org`, LinkedIn `/company/comvivo`, and — once the
   domain exists — the self-verifying Bluesky **domain handle** `@comvivo.dev`. All verified free.

**Re-verified 2026-11-03 (`M0-T15`).** Still free, checked against the registries directly rather than from memory:
`comvivo`, `create-comvivo`, `@comvivo/server`, `@comvivo/plugin-sdk` and `@comvivo/channel-sdk` all return **404**
from `registry.npmjs.org`, and `github.com/comvivo` returns **404**. This also **resolves the blocking finding in
`12` §3.1**: the unscoped name under the old codename was taken, which meant `npx <name> init` could not ship. It is
free under Comvivo, so the one-command install works as `00` §3.7 promises.

**Draft issue, ready to file.** Copy this into a new issue on `faizannraza/comvivo` — I cannot create accounts or
file on your behalf. Title: **Reserve the Comvivo distribution names**.

```markdown
Every name in `deploy/names.json` is still unregistered as of 2026-11-03. Securing them is the only
squatting risk in the project, because the repository is public and the name is decided
(ADR-024).

Nothing here needs code. Each item is an account action only you can perform.

- [ ] **GitHub organisation `comvivo`** — <https://github.com/organizations/plan> → Free.
      This also claims `ghcr.io/comvivo`, the container namespace the release pipeline publishes to.
      Then transfer this repository into the org (Settings → Danger Zone → Transfer). GitHub keeps
      redirects, and `BRAND.repo` plus `deploy/names.json` are the only lines that change —
      `pnpm names:check` fails until both are updated, and `pnpm names:apply` does the rewrite.
- [ ] **npm organisation `comvivo`** — `npm login`, then `npm org create comvivo`. Free for public
      packages. This claims the `@comvivo` scope.
- [ ] **Unscoped `comvivo`** and **`create-comvivo`** — needed for `npx comvivo@latest init` and
      `npm create comvivo`, which are the documented install paths.
- [ ] **Defensive `convivo` and `create-convivo`** — English-first speakers reach for `con-`.
- [ ] **Enable "Require 2FA and disallow tokens"** on the npm organisation. The release pipeline
      uses OIDC trusted publishing and holds no `NPM_TOKEN`, so this costs nothing and closes the
      stolen-token path entirely.

**Do not publish placeholder packages.** npm discourages name squatting, and `13` §3.A.4 says real
alpha builds ship at M8. Claiming the org and the scope reserves the namespace without publishing.

**Timing note for npm trusted publishing (`M12-T90`):** the first publish must happen within two
days of configuring the trusted publisher, so configure that at M12, not now.
```

**Also worth doing, but not blocking:** the formal trademark registers could not be searched without a human. Every one
refused automated access — USPTO's search is an interactive SPA with no documented wordmark field, TSDR needs an API
key, EUIPO/TMview sits behind a WAF, WIPO behind a proof-of-work challenge, UKIPO behind a CAPTCHA. **Trademarkia
returned zero results for `comvivo`**, which is a strong substitute but **not a clearance opinion**. If you want the
real thing: run `comvivo` in classes 9, 38, 42 and 45 at https://tmsearch.uspto.gov, https://www.tmdn.org/tmview/ and
https://branddb.wipo.int/, and optionally buy a one-hour attorney knockout opinion (money, offered not required). The
one residual worth raising with an attorney is VIVO, the handset maker, which holds class-9 marks — COMVIVO is a
unitary coined word rather than "Vivo" with a prefix, and *vivo* is a weak common Romance word used by many unrelated
brands, but it is the only adjacency found.

## Decisions log

Every significant delegated decision, with its rationale. Architectural decisions also get an ADR in `docs/adr/`.

| # | Date | Decision | Rationale | ADR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| DL-1 | 2026-11-01 | **D-3 — repo stays public but unannounced.** | The owner created the repository as public and instructed that it stay unannounced until the 1.0 launch gates pass. `15`'s recommendation was "public unannounced at M8", so this is the same posture, reached earlier. Consequence: `M3-T52` edge images must push to a namespace that is not advertised, and `13` §3.A.3's name shortlist is redacted from the public tree so the candidates cannot be squatted. | — |
| DL-2 | 2026-11-01 | **D-8 — WhatsApp stays out of 1.x.** | `06` §B's conclusion is unchanged: Meta's WhatsApp Business Platform does not support bots as members of ordinary user groups, and its AI-assistant policy is restrictive. Unofficial clients (Baileys, whatsapp-web.js) are a permanent "never" (`00` §6.3) on terms-of-service and legal grounds. | — |
| DL-3 | 2026-11-01 | **D-5 — resources-only response to minors' disclosures; no automatic guardian alert.** | This is the safest design and the one every option in `15` D-5 includes, so building it exclusively forecloses nothing. An automatic alert can out a child to the person harming them, and Village cannot verify who a "guardian" actually is. Building no escalation code path at all is also the only way to be sure one cannot be triggered by prompt injection. A paid expert review is offered but never gates the release; the automated substitute is a dedicated red-team family plus an honest documented limitation. | pending (M5) |
| DL-4 | 2026-11-01 | **D-7 — GitHub Discussions is the community baseline.** | It needs no new account, no hosting, no moderation bot and no spend, and it is indexable, which matters for support SEO. Discord is additive and is handed to the owner as a short set of steps at M8. Running Village in its own community is excellent dogfooding but is real money, so it carries a proposed cap. | — |
| DL-8 | 2026-11-01 | **D-2 — the product is `Comvivo`.** Two earlier choices (**Folkstead**, **Commonry**) were made and reversed on the way. | *com* (with) + *vivo* (I live) = "living together", the root of *convivial*: the product's thesis and its tone target in one coined word. Zero Trademarkia results, zero prior software use, and every surface free — npm `comvivo` and `create-comvivo`, the `@comvivo` scope, the typo-space (`convivo`), the GitHub org (so `ghcr.io/comvivo`), zero matching repositories, `.dev`/`.org`/`.sh`/`.ai`/`.app`, PyPI, crates.io, Hugging Face, Mastodon, LinkedIn, Bluesky.<br><br>**All four `13` §3.A.3 shortlisted names failed**, three because an active product already occupies this exact category under senior common-law rights that no trademark register records — the failure mode a registry-only check cannot see.<br><br>**Reversal 1 — Folkstead.** It cleared every availability and trademark check perfectly and was decided, documented and applied. It was reversed within the hour on a register finding surfaced by the Folkmoot report: "folkish" (German *völkisch*) is the self-applied label of the racist branch of Germanic Heathenry, and **"stead" is a documented Heathen term for a kindred** (Asatru Alliance: "Kindreds are sometimes called a garth, **stead**, or skeppslag"), with "folk-" as that movement's marker prefix (cf. the Asatru **Folk** Assembly; the SPLC's "Neo-Völkisch" category). No actual far-right use of "folkstead" exists — the risk is a latent reading — but it is unfixable by explanation, it lands on a product whose whole pitch is inclusive shared belonging, and avoiding it cost one hour before any product code existed.<br><br>**Reversal 2 — Commonry.** Clean on npm and the GitHub org, then found to have a **live registered COMMONRY mark in International Class 009** (downloadable software) plus a live-pending one in class 045. This is where the method was corrected: **screen trademark and same-category products first, availability second** — those two checks are the only ones that kill a name outright and both are cheap.<br><br>**Two findings supersede `13` §3.A.2–§3.A.3 for any future rename.** (1) The entire Anglo-Saxon/Norse revivalist register is off-limits: *folk-*, *moot*, *stead*, *garth*, *hearth*, *kindred*, *thing*, *frith*, *troth*, *thorp*, *kith*, *kin*. §3.A.3's whole candidate family drew from that one well, which is why waves 1 and 2 both failed. (2) Every apt dictionary word or obvious compound in this space is already claimed by someone building something adjacent, so **only a genuine coinage is reliably free** — which is also the strongest mark class, and what Immich, Jellyfin, Forgejo, Traefik, Umami and Ollama all did. | [ADR-024](../adr/ADR-024-product-name.md) |
| DL-9 | 2026-11-01 | **TypeScript is pinned to 6.0.3, not 7.x.** | `typescript-eslint@8.72.1` declares a hard peer of `typescript >=4.8.4 <6.1.0`, and TS 7 support is closed `not_planned` upstream pending a stable TS 7 JavaScript API (typescript-eslint#12518, #10940; TS 7.0.2 ships no `tsserver` binary and no `lib/typescript.js`). The four type-aware rules the spine makes non-negotiable — `no-floating-promises`, `no-misused-promises`, `switch-exhaustiveness-check`, `strict-boolean-expressions` — only exist there; Biome's equivalents are nursery-grade with `information` severity and cannot carry a gate. A working lint gate on an event-driven server is worth more than being on the newest compiler, so TS 6 it is. Revisit when typescript-eslint#12803 (TS 7.1 native-parser support over IPC) lands. | [ADR-025](../adr/ADR-025-typescript-version-pin.md) |
| DL-10 | 2026-11-01 | **pnpm settings live in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, and `allowBuilds` replaces `onlyBuiltDependencies`.** | pnpm 11 moved every setting except auth/registry/TLS/proxy out of `.npmrc`, and pnpm 11 **removed** `onlyBuiltDependencies`, `neverBuiltDependencies`, `ignoredBuiltDependencies` and `ignoreDepScripts` in favour of the `allowBuilds` *map*. `strictDepBuilds` now defaults to `true`, so any package with an install script that is not listed hard-fails the install — which is exactly the behaviour `08` §3.15 wanted and had to ask for explicitly. `minimumReleaseAge` is in **minutes** (4320 = 3 days) and already defaults to 1440. Corepack is gone from Node ≥ 25, and pnpm now reads `devEngines.packageManager` itself, governed by `pmOnFail`. `08` §3.15's YAML block is updated to the current spelling. | — |
| DL-11 | 2026-11-01 | **`--allow-net` cannot implement the guarded-egress allowlist; the in-process allowlist stays load-bearing.** | `01` §14 A1 treats `--allow-net` (Node ≥ 25) as the plugin network-isolation mechanism and records weaker isolation on Node 24 as the residual risk. Verified on Node 26: the flag is **boolean, all-or-nothing, with no host or port scoping** (`AddOption("--allow-net", …, BOOL_FIELD(allow_net))`; per-host scoping is only a proposal, nodejs/node#67115). So on *every* Node version, invariant **I5 (guarded egress)** must be enforced by `shared/net`'s `safeFetch` allowlist, and `--allow-net` is only a coarse second layer. This strengthens rather than weakens A1, and the residual-risk wording in `08` is corrected accordingly. | — |
| DL-5 | 2026-11-01 | **pnpm 12.16.1, not pnpm 11.** | `08` §3.15 and `01` §14 A1 require "pnpm ≥ 11, pinned by `packageManager`, installed explicitly, never Corepack". pnpm 12 is the current major, satisfies "≥ 11", and the supply-chain settings the plan depends on (`minimumReleaseAge`, `trustPolicy`, build allowlisting) are present and better documented there. Logged as a Plan change rather than a spine change because the spec states a floor, not a pin. | — |
| DL-6 | 2026-11-01 | **Node pins: 26.13.0 primary, 24.22.2 matrix leg.** | `01` §14 A1 names "Node 26 primary, Node 24 ≥ 24.11 supported". 26.13.0 is the Active LTS ("Lithium", since 2026-10-28) and 24.22.2 is the newest 24.x, so these are the concrete versions behind A1. `.node-version` holds `26` so minor bumps need no commit. | — |
| DL-7 | 2026-11-01 | **Homebrew is not used for the toolchain.** | Homebrew 6.0.12 reports this macOS/CLT combination as a Tier-3 (unsupported) configuration and therefore builds every formula from source, which failed for both `pnpm` and `fnm`. Official Node tarballs plus an explicit global `pnpm` install are reproducible and match what CI does. Recorded so a future session does not retry brew. | — |

| 2026-11-03 | M0-T21 | `11` §3.8.3 | **A required status check whose job is event-conditional blocks the merge forever.** Measured: on a push `scan-all / osv-scan` succeeded while `scan-pr` reported `skipped`; on a pull request it is the reverse. GitHub shows a never-reporting check as *pending*, not passed, and nothing times out. `osv.yml` and `zizmor.yml` now end in aggregators with stable names (`osv-ok`, `zizmor-ok`), the same pattern as `ci-ok` | `osv-ok` additionally fails if **neither** scan ran, because "nothing was checked" must not look like "nothing was wrong". Without this the merge queue would have stalled on its first use, in a way that reads as a hung check rather than a misconfiguration |
| 2026-11-03 | M0-T21 | `11` §3.8.3 | **An aggregator fixes job-level conditionality, not workflow-level triggering.** `zizmor.yml` filtered its `pull_request` trigger on `.github/**`, so a pull request touching nothing there never started the workflow — and `zizmor-ok` therefore never reported either. The filter is off `pull_request` and stays on `push` | The aggregator I had just added for exactly this class of problem could not help, because there was no run to aggregate. Worth knowing before adding a `paths` filter to any workflow whose checks are required; now documented in `docs/architecture/ci.md` |
| 2026-11-03 | M0-T21 | `11` §3.8.3 | **The merge queue cannot be enabled: it requires an organisation-owned repository.** `POST /rulesets` with a `merge_queue` rule returns `422 Invalid rule 'merge_queue':` with an empty detail, on every combination tried — alone, with the `pull_request` rule, with `MERGE` instead of `SQUASH`, enforcement active and disabled. A complete parameter set passes the schema and is then rejected as a rule. Settings documented for the day the repo moves into the `comvivo` org (**H-6**) | Measured rather than assumed, and the shape of the failure is the evidence: schema-valid, rule-rejected, no detail — a feature gate rather than a bad request. `comvivo` is user-owned on a personal Pro plan. Meanwhile **"require branches to be up to date" is on**, which is the manual equivalent of the property the queue would guarantee by building each entry on top of `main`; it is deliberately off in the queued configuration, where the queue owns that job |
| 2026-11-03 | M0-T21 | `11` §3.8.3 | **Zero approving reviews are required**, and `.github/CODEOWNERS` is complete but not enforced | `11` §3.8.3 asks for one review, and two on the security paths. The checks are what gate a merge today, which means a change waits on CI rather than on somebody being awake. When a second maintainer arrives this is one settings change — which is why CODEOWNERS was written out in full at `M0-T17` rather than deferred |

Replaced with content detection (a NUL byte in the first 8 KiB, the heuristic git uses); the extension list is gone | The project's recurring failure shape, this time inside a tool built to catch it. Found by scanning the renamed tree by hand instead of trusting the report, which is the only reason it was visible. Content detection cannot miss a text file, which is the direction that matters: a false "binary" is a silent gap, a false "text" at worst rewrites nothing. Skipped-file count went from 3 to **0** |

Free under Comvivo. Checked directly rather than carried forward from the naming decision, because `12` §3.1 states it as blocking and a stale "it was free on Sunday" is not evidence |

Changed to `privatePackages: { version: true, tag: false }` | The spec's intent — private workspaces get no tags and are never published — is preserved exactly. What is not expressible is `version: false`, because a published package cannot be bumped when a private dependency it bundles changes. The private versions are internal bookkeeping that never leaves the repository; `12` §3.10.4's `npm-pack` check (no `workspace:` in a packed manifest) is what makes that safe, since the published manifests do not list these dependencies at all |

The other 20 workspaces stay private | `12` §3.10.1 designates exactly these four as published, and `M0-T1` had scaffolded everything private — so the `fixed` group and the independent SDK versioning were inert. Accidental-publish risk is covered by there being no publish path and no npm credential anywhere in the repository (`release.yml` holds no `id-token` or registry login), with the trusted publisher arriving at `M12-T90` |

Our own check can be, and is, matching every other guard in this tree — a pure policy module with 36 tests plus a thin runner. Also: the formatter makes **no network call**, unlike `@changesets/changelog-github`, which resolves pull requests through the API and fails without a token. | 2026-11-03 | M1-T2 | `02` §3.6.2, §3.6.3, §3.14; `12` §3.7 | **The database file and the process lock are renamed before either has ever been written**: `village.db` → `comvivo.db` (7 places) and `village.lock` → `comvivo.lock` (4), plus the spike canary `VILLAGE-CANARY-7f3a` → `COMVIVO-CANARY-7f3a` | Same class as `kind='assistant'`: a brand-named **file in a host's data directory** turns a rename into a migration, and `02` §3.14's backup manifest has it as a `z.literal('db/village.db')`, so it would have been frozen into every `VBK1` archive ever written. Free today, expensive after `M1-T7` and `M1-T27`. **Deliberately not renamed:** the `VBK1`/`VBL1` envelope magic and the `.vbak` extension — magic bytes are opaque tokens whose only requirements are uniqueness and stability, neither of which the letter `V` threatens, and stability is the property a file format actually wants. The remaining `village.*` names (the `village.service` unit, the `11` §3.10.2 metric and span namespaces, the CLI's own filenames) are renamed by the milestone that creates each one, the same way, rather than in one sweep now — a rename landing with the code that first writes the artefact is reviewable; a 100-identifier sweep across nine specs is not |

| 2026-11-04 | M1-T2 | `02` §3.6.2, §3.16.4 | **`cacheSizeKiB` now defaults to 192 MiB, and the spec says it is not a tuning knob.** With SQLCipher, a KNN query whose vector index does not fit in the page cache re-reads and **re-decrypts the whole partition**: measured **508 ms p50** at 50k × 384-d with an 8 MiB cache against **5 ms** with 192 MiB — a 100× cliff from one pragma. The index is ~1.6 KiB per vector (77 MiB at 50k). `09` §C gains a `comvivo doctor` warning when the cache is smaller than the largest vector partition | The small-cache figure is **8× outside** `02` §3.16.4's 60 ms KNN budget and the large one is twelve times inside it, so this is the difference between memory search working and not. I went looking for a bug in the benchmark first; there was none. Two things make it worth a spec change rather than a comment: the failure is invisible (the symptom is "search feels slow", with nothing in the logs and nothing in a test), and above the cliff the **encrypted** build is *faster* than the plaintext one (5 ms against 15 ms), so nobody would suspect encryption |
| 2026-11-04 | M1-T2 | `02` §3.6.3 step 6 | **Drizzle cannot load against `better-sqlite3-multiple-ciphers` at all without a dependency alias.** `drizzle-orm/better-sqlite3/driver.js` does a top-level `import 'better-sqlite3'`, so the module throws `Cannot find package 'better-sqlite3'` before any type question arises. `packages/storage/package.json` declares `"better-sqlite3": "npm:better-sqlite3-multiple-ciphers@13.0.3"`, and `pins.test.ts` asserts the aliased version **equals the catalog pin** so the two cannot become two copies of SQLite in one process | The spec anticipated a *type* problem — "a cast to `BetterSqlite3.Database` is acceptable" — and the real problem was module resolution. With the alias the two type names are the same installed package, so **no cast is needed either**: the spec's permitted workaround turned out to be unnecessary once the actual cause was fixed |

Also: a KNN query may only `ORDER BY distance`; a rowid must bind as a `BigInt` because better-sqlite3 binds a plain `number` as a float; and vec0 metadata `UPDATE` **is** supported | §3.6.2 kept `trusted_schema` ON because this was "unverified"; it is now verified, and ON is the only setting under which a retracted memory's vector is actually deleted — the OFF failure mode is a trigger that looks installed and throws on first use. | 2026-11-04 | M1-T2 | `11` §3.1.3, `01` §4 | **A test that imported a sibling workspace package passed locally and failed in CI**, because `@comvivo/shared` resolved through `node_modules` into a `dist/` my machine happened to have: `Failed to resolve entry for package "@comvivo/shared"` on seven spike files and `pins.test.ts`. The `@comvivo/*` → `src/` map moved out of `tsconfig.depcruise.json` into a new **`tsconfig.paths.json`**, which `tsconfig.depcruise.json` and `tsconfig.test.json` both extend and which `vitest.shared.ts` reads to build `resolve.alias`. `pnpm verify` is now green **from a tree with no `dist/` anywhere** | These were the **first three cross-package imports in this repository's history** — nothing had exercised workspace resolution in a test before, so the gap was always there and I was the first to stand on it. Same shape as M0-T18's `.astro` types: a local check weaker than CI's while printing the same result. One file rather than three copies, because the failure is silent in all three directions — dependency-cruiser reports success over an unresolved edge, `tsc` reports `any`, and Vitest fails only on a machine with no `dist/`, which is a fresh checkout and CI and never the machine you are working on. `baseUrl` was dropped in the move: it is deprecated in TS 6 and removed in 7, and a `paths` entry without one resolves relative to the config that declares it |

| 2026-11-04 | M1-T3 | `02` §3.1, `11` §3.1.7 | **The `IdGenerator` port took the brand as a separate argument, so an id could be branded as the wrong entity.** `generate<B extends string>(prefix: IdPrefix, brand?: B)` with `IdPrefix = string` meant `generate('grp', 'member')` typechecked and produced a group id typed as a `MemberId`. Replaced with `02` §3.1's shape: `next<P extends IdPrefix>(prefix: P): Id<P>`, where `IdPrefix` is the closed union of the registry's 61 values and the brand **is** the prefix | The whole argument for branded ids is that operating on the wrong group or the wrong member is invariant **I4** or **I3** failing silently, and the old shape let exactly that mistake typecheck. The closed union found two real instances immediately: the `M0-T7` suites were passing `'turn'` and `'out'` — **entity names**, where the prefixes are `trn` and `obx` — so the property suite had been minting ids no parser would ever accept and asserting their ordering with great rigour. Both are now compile errors |
| 2026-11-04 | M1-T3 | `02` §3.1 | `zod.ts`'s `brandedId` is **removed** in favour of `ids.ts`'s `idSchema` | Same defect as the port: it took prefix and brand independently. Its pattern was also wrong in a second way — `[0-9a-hjkmnp-tv-z]{26}` accepts a suffix starting `8`–`z`, which decodes past 128 bits and which `typeid-js` refuses. So it validated ids that could be written and then never read back. `idSchema` takes the prefix from the registry, uses the same parser as storage, and reports the same message, so an API error and a storage error say the same thing about the same string |
| 2026-11-04 | M1-T3 | `02` §3.1 | **File layout:** the registry and parser are `shared/src/ids.ts` as specified, but the UUIDv7 byte layout is factored into `shared/src/uuidv7.ts` and the generator into `shared/src/id-generator.ts` | `M0-T7`'s `DeterministicIdGenerator` already had its own copy of the RFC 9562 §5.7 layout, and the production generator would have been a second. Two copies could drift so that **test ids sorted and production ids did not** — or the reverse, which is worse, because the suite would be green while the turn journal and the outbox (invariants I6, I7) silently reordered. One `UuidV7Source`, used by both, with a test asserting they produce the same timestamp and the same version and variant bits |
| 2026-11-04 | M1-T3 | `08` §3.7.1, PSC-8 | **`shared/src/crypto/random.ts` lands early, in M1-T3 rather than M1-T4.** The id generator needs a CSPRNG, and `crypto-only-in-shared-crypto-and-storage-crypto` correctly refused `import 'node:crypto'` from `id-generator.ts` | The alternatives were to widen the rule (making "cryptography lives in two reviewed modules" false) or to thread a `randomBytes` function through the generator's constructor to satisfy a path regex. Writing the module the rule already expects is the only one of the three that leaves the architecture intact. It also means `08` §3.7.12's typed codes and base64url tokens exist from the start, with `randomTypedCode` drawing against the alphabet's real length rather than reducing a byte modulo 32 — unbiased today only because 32 divides 256, and a trap the moment somebody changes the alphabet |
| 2026-11-04 | M1-T3 | `11` §3.1.4, RFC 8785 | **RFC 8785's Appendix B is a 100-million-line vector file hosted outside this repository, and `pnpm verify` does not download it.** The substitution, stated rather than glossed: §3.2.3's worked example verbatim; number serialisation cross-checked against **`JSON.stringify`**, which is the normative reference §3.2.2.3 points at — so for the number path this is the Appendix B oracle itself, sampled by a property test over random doubles including denormals and extremes; and one dedicated test for each of the three places the implementation must **not** delegate | The number format is the only part of JCS where a fixed vector list is the usual evidence, and it is also the part the platform already implements correctly, so a property test against the platform is stronger than a sampled list. The three deviations are where delegation stops and they each have a reason: `-0` must serialise as `0` or two equal values hash differently; `NaN` and `±Infinity` must be refused rather than becoming `null`, which would give a corrupt value a valid hash; and a **lone surrogate** must pass through, where ES2019's well-formed `JSON.stringify` escapes it — lone surrogates reach this product for real, from a platform that truncated a message mid-emoji |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T4 | `02` §3.6.1, `08` §3.7.4, §3.7.10 | **The specs' cryptographic domain separators still carried the dead codename**, and `M1-T5` is the last moment they are free to change: `info="village/v1/" + purpose` (8 places), `"village-keyring/1"` (3), `"village/blob-dek/v1|"` (3) and `"village/blob/v1|"` (1) — 15 in all, now `comvivo`. The file magics `VBL1` and `VBK1` are **deliberately left alone** | A domain separator has no migration: change one after a host has data and their database stops opening, their blobs stop decrypting and their backups stop restoring, all at once and silently. So this had to happen before `M1-T5` writes the first keyring, or never. The magics are the opposite case — a magic number's only requirements are uniqueness and stability, the letter `V` threatens neither, and stability is precisely the property a file format wants |

Corrected to the spec | Without the instance id, a keyring slot copied from one host's `keyring.json` into another's would have unwrapped. Transcribing a security constant is exactly where a glance is not good enough |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T4 | `02` M1-T4 AC, `08` PSC-8 | **Primitive files live in `shared/crypto`, not `storage/src/crypto`** — the deviation `02` M1-T4's AC already anticipated ("logged as Plan change"). The deciding argument is that `shared` is the bottom of the graph: `08` §3.7.12's token formats and the magic-link MAC are needed by `apps/server` and the CLI, which must not import `storage` to get a HMAC |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T4 | `02` D13, `08` §3.7.1 | **`sodium-native` is implemented now, as a devDependency with a dynamic import, rather than when SP-2 comes in.** `02` D13 calls the fallback "wire-identical" and `08` §3.7.1 says "behind the same interface"; `stream.contract.test.ts` runs the whole `SecretStream` contract against **both** implementations (46 tests) and then seals with one and opens with the other, in both directions | Two claims that were worth nothing untested, and one of them turned out to need work: `sodium-native`'s secretstream API is **not** a drop-in — it takes caller-allocated output buffers and an explicit state buffer where `libsodium-wrappers` returns values and hides state, and it throws where the WASM build sometimes returns `false`. Finding that out now cost twenty minutes; finding it out on the day a Pi 5 benchmark came in badly would have cost a decision already made. The import is dynamic so `no-dev-dep-in-src` stays satisfied until D13 actually flips |

The protection that replaces it is **structural**: `labels.test.ts` reads `labels.ts` with comments stripped and asserts it imports nothing and never mentions `BRAND`. That matters because every value assertion would pass if the file were rewritten to interpolate `BRAND.slug` — `BRAND.slug` *is* `comvivo` today — and the damage would appear only at the next rename, in a shipped release. Extracting the size guard was the better change anyway — it is the premise of the whole fallback, so being able to demonstrate that the check works is worth more than an inline `if` nobody can exercise |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T4 | `14` §2 M0-T23 AC, `13` §3.A.6 | **Two correct rules collided the moment a task title contained the npm scope.** `STATUS.md` is in the rename codemod's HISTORY list and is deliberately not rewritten — its body is the record of what things used to be called — while `14-milestones.md` **is** rewritten. `M1-T1`'s title is literally `` `@comvivo/storage` scaffold & pins `` and `M12-T19`'s is `` `@comvivo/mcp-bridge` ``, so after a rename the plan said `@zzbrand/storage`, STATUS still said `@comvivo/storage`, and `trace.py` reported `E-STATUS-TITLE` drift that did not exist. `scope_agnostic()` now normalises every npm scope to `@*/` on both sides before comparing | **Found by the weekly rename drill on the M1 pull request** — the third time that job has paid for itself, and the first time it caught something no amount of reading would have: the conflict is invisible until a title contains a scope, which no M0 task's did. Normalising is the right fix rather than a workaround because of the division of labour: this checker's job is **drift** between STATUS and the plan, and `pnpm names:check` already owns whether every published name agrees with `BRAND`. A title differing only by scope is not drift; a title differing by a word or by the package after the scope still fails, and both directions are tested. Re-verified by running the whole drill locally: rename, install, `pnpm verify` green with 1554 tests, and `pnpm --filter @zzbrand/docs build` green |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T5 | `08` §3.7.4 | **`sealed` was validated for presence, not for shape, and that turned damage into "wrong key".** The slot schema was `z.string().min(1)`; it is now unpadded-base64url plus an exact decoded length of `sealedLength(32)` = 60 bytes, with a matching check in `openSlot` for keyrings built in memory | **`Buffer.from(s, 'base64url')` never throws** — it silently drops characters outside the alphabet, so `'!!!!'` decodes to zero bytes and `'ab~cd'` to three. A corrupted `sealed` field therefore reached `unwrap`, came back as an ordinary `UnwrapFailed`, and the host was told *"the key does not match this keyring… recover with your recovery key"* when the truth was *"this file is damaged, restore `keyring.json.prev`"* — sending them down a destructive path they may not even be able to walk, for a one-command problem. `unwrap` cannot make the distinction itself: it reports every failure identically on purpose, because a caller that could tell "wrong key" from "wrong AAD" would have an oracle. Here there is none to protect — the keyring is a local, host-owned file — so the check belongs at the boundary, where `CLAUDE.md` §2 puts it. Found by chasing an **uncovered line**: the `try`/`catch` around `Buffer.from` could not be reached, and the reason it could not be reached was the bug. Four tests, two at `openSlot` and two at `parseKeyring`. It also subsumes `key-service.ts`'s post-unwrap length guard — AES-GCM preserves length, so a 60-byte wrap can only yield 32 bytes — which is now documented as defence in depth rather than quietly left as missed coverage |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T5 | `08` §3.7.2, §3.7.11 | **`KeyService` threw when unlocking a retired generation**, which is the one thing retired generations exist for. `08` §3.7.2 wraps the static keys under the **current** RDK only, so an old generation genuinely cannot unwrap `static.blobId`. Added `hasBlobIdKey`, and a hint on `blobIdKey` pointing at §3.7.11's backup snapshot | Would have surfaced at `M1-T27` as "restoring an old backup fails with `KEYRING_MALFORMED`" — a misdiagnosis during a restore, which is the worst possible moment. A boolean rather than a caught exception because reading an old backup legitimately cannot produce the key, and a `try`/`catch` around a normal condition is how a real error later gets swallowed. §3.7.11's `keyringSnapshot.static.blobId` is **required** precisely so a restore can recompute blob addresses, so the honest answer is "not from this unlock" plus where to get it |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T5 | `08` §3.7.3, §3.7.4 | **File layout:** the atomic write, the `.prev` rotation and the permission audit are `storage/src/crypto/keyring-store.ts`, not inside `keyring.ts` as `14` §2's file list implies; the injected `KeyringIo` and its fake live with them | `sec.keys.*` has to prove crash-resume, and the interesting failures are *between* two syscalls — after the temp file is fsynced but before the rename, after the live file is moved aside but before the new one lands. That needs file I/O to be a parameter, and a seam wide enough to mock the whole of `node:fs` would be wide enough to make the tests meaningless. Splitting the policy (`keyring.ts`: schema, slots, AAD) from the I/O (`keyring-store.ts`) makes the fake small and faithful: five fault points, each asserted to leave a host with a working keyring |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T5 | `08` §3.7.3 line 1287 | The spec's config example still read `keychainService: village`; corrected to `comvivo`, and the implementation derives the default from `BRAND.slug` with the config key as the override. `keychain.ts` exports `DEFAULT_KEYCHAIN_SERVICE` and `osKeychain(service, load)` rather than a fixed-name singleton | A bare-word machine-readable value, so the 2026-11-03 sweep (which matched `@village/`, `VILLAGE_`, `village.yaml`) could not see it — the class that sweep's own note says is renamed by the milestone that creates the artefact. It is **not** a frozen wire constant like the HKDF and AAD labels: it is a lookup key the host can see and change, which is exactly what makes a rename survivable here — the keychain entry keeps the name it was created under, so a host upgrading across a rename points the new build at the old entry instead of losing the master key |
| 2026-11-05 | M1-T5 | `11` §3.1.4 | **The real adapters were untested while the fakes were fully covered.** `nodeUnlockIo` sat at 58% function coverage with every §3.7.5 ordering rule green, and `keychain.ts` had no test at all. Added `unlock-sources.int.test.ts` (18 tests, real env vars and real files) and `keychain.test.ts` (17, loader injected). Both now 100% | M0's recurring lesson, and this subsystem's own precedent: `syncDirectory` opened a directory with `r+` and failed with `EISDIR` on Linux, which no in-memory fake would ever have noticed. A fake can be wrong in the same direction as the code. Two real behaviours were only documentable once the real adapter ran: `fileExists` answers **false** for a directory and for an unreadable file, so a permission problem is indistinguishable from a missing one through that interface — which is why the `MASTER_KEY_NOT_FOUND` hint names the path and `auditPermissions` is what actually diagnoses it. `keychain.ts`'s loader is injected for the same reason `KeyringIo` is: the real one prompts on macOS, fails headless, and would leave a live credential on whichever machine it passed |

A second defect hid inside the first: `grep "'[^']*\${BRAND\."` reported the single-quote form gone when **eight** hints still had it, because the apostrophes broke the pattern too — so for a while the guard was green while hints would have printed `${BRAND.cliName}` literally to users. The `switch` over `Verdict` names every case and has **no** `default`: with every case listed, `switch-exhaustiveness-check` is itself the `never` check `CLAUDE.md` §2 asks for, and it refuses a `default` because one would hide the next addition |

So a value between the two **passed the guard** and then threw a raw `RangeError` out of `fromEpochMilliseconds`. `MAX_EPOCH_MS` is now the measured limit | A bound that admits values the library refuses is worse than no bound: the guard exists to turn a bad value into a `StorageError` with a hint, and in that window it turned one into an unmapped `RangeError` from inside a column mapper — which no caller catches and which names neither the column nor the row. Found by a round-trip test over the boundary values, which is the only kind that would notice: every value a real clock produces is twelve orders of magnitude below either limit |
| 2026-11-06 | M1-T10 | `02` §3.1 | **`tzText` canonicalises on write, not only on read.** `02` §3.1 says "validated via Temporal on read"; measured, Temporal **normalises case** — `utc` → `UTC`, `EUROPE/LONDON` → `Europe/London` — so validating only on read lets `europe/london` into a column | And `02` D3's calendar design compares a stored `tz` against another `tz` to decide whether a derived `*_at` is still valid, so two spellings of one zone is a **stale-derivation** bug: the derived instant is never refreshed because the zone "changed", or is refreshed forever because it never matches. Neither symptom points at the column. `shared/src/time/timezone.ts` owns the rule so `07`'s recurrence code and this column get the same answer from the same tzdata, and `timeZoneIdSchema` **transforms** rather than refines — a refining schema would accept `europe/london` and pass it through unchanged, which is exactly how two spellings reach the database. Link names (`US/Eastern`) are deliberately **not** collapsed: Temporal keeps them, and `02` D3 stores what the host chose |
| 2026-11-06 | M1-T10 | `02` §3.1 | **I had the `bool` hazard backwards.** I wrote that a `bool()` column without its `CHECK` reads `2` back as `true`. Measured on drizzle-orm 0.45.4: `{ mode: 'boolean' }` maps `1` to `true` and **everything else to `false`** — `2`, `−1` and `0.5` all read `false` | Which makes the `CHECK` *more* important, not less. A value that reached the column around the mapper does not read as nonsense a caller might notice; it reads as a confident `false`, indistinguishable from a real one. For `is_active`, `is_minor` or a consent flag that is the dangerous direction — a consent flag reading `false` silently denies. The `CHECK` is the only layer that can stop the value existing, and `boolCheck` builds the expression so no table spells it out. Caught by a test asserting my own claim and failing: **the fourth time this session** that measuring corrected a stated rationale rather than the code |
| 2026-11-06 | M1-T10 | `02` §3.1 | `labelRef` returns the typed integer column **without** `.references(() => labels.id)`, which `02` §3.1 writes inline | `_columns.ts` cannot import the `labels` table: `labels` is declared in a module that imports `_columns.ts`, so the reference would be a module cycle — and dependency-cruiser is right to refuse one between the column vocabulary and the tables built from it, because the import order would then decide whether the schema loads at all. The foreign key is declared by the table, which is also where `02` §3.1's `ON DELETE NO ACTION` for labels belongs; `M1-T11` adds it and reviews the generated SQL for it |
| 2026-11-06 | M1-T10 | `02` §3.2 | `LabelId` and `ReaderSetId` are **branded integers** (`InternedId<Table>`) in `shared/src/ids.ts`, alongside the TypeID machinery rather than in it | `02` §3.2's labels and reader sets are *interned* — one row per distinct value per group — and are integers where every aggregate above them is a TypeID. Three reasons, and the first decides it: **every labeled row carries a `label_id`**, so it is among the most repeated values in the database, and a 30-character TypeID against a 4-byte integer is a real difference in index size — which `02` §3.6.2 measured as a 100× difference on vector search once the index stops fitting in the page cache. They are also never exposed (a TypeID's value is being safe in a URL or a log; a label id appears in neither), and interning needs a cheap identity for its `Map` cache. Branded separately because both are integers from the same kind of source, which is exactly when a brand earns its place: the mistake typechecks otherwise and the symptom is a row labeled with somebody else's audience |
| 2026-11-06 | M1-T10 | `02` §3.1 | `instantMs` **refuses** sub-millisecond precision rather than truncating it | `02` §3.1 says `Clock.now()` truncates to ms, so an instant arriving with nanoseconds came from code that bypassed the clock port. Truncating silently would make two distinct instants compare equal after a round trip, and the turn journal and the outbox read timestamps **as sequence** (I6, I7) — so a silent truncation is an ordering bug that appears only under load. `epochNanoseconds % 1_000_000n` is the only way to see the precision at all, because `epochMilliseconds` has already discarded it by the time you can read it |

The opposite extreme is worse: `jsonOf` validates on **read**, so an unvalidated column cannot report corruption, and an uncapped one can be grown until it breaks a context window or a backup. The cap is real and enforced today. | 2026-11-07 | audit | `M1-T7` AC, `12` §3.7 | **The filesystem check could not work on macOS, and "could not tell" was reported as "fine".** `MAGIC_TYPES` is a table of Linux `linux/magic.h` constants; macOS's `statfs` `f_type` is a small **type index** — measured on darwin 23.6: `26` for APFS (`/`, `/Users`, `/private/tmp`, `/System/Volumes/Data`) and `19` for devfs. So nothing in the table could ever match, every darwin verdict came from the sync-marker check alone, and `verdictFor` turned an undetermined type into `ok`. An SMB or NFS data directory on a Mac — the case `12` §3.7 exists for — reported clean | The same defect as the 9p magic bug in a different place: not knowing, reported as fine. Three changes. `typeFromMagic` is **platform-aware**, because the number means a Linux magic on one platform and a type index on the other — reading one table on the other platform cannot be right even where it happens to be harmless (every Linux magic exceeds `0x100`, so no darwin index could collide; that was luck, not design). `DARWIN_FS_TYPES` holds only the two values measurable on real hardware — guessing an `smbfs` index would be the unverified table entry that caused the original bug. And `FsVerdict` gains **`unknown`**, which warns: `12` §3.7's rule is warn at boot and error in `doctor`, never refuse, so an undetermined type belongs on the warning side of that line. The in-code comment claiming "`statfs` plus the APFS/HFS magics cover the local cases" was false in both halves and is corrected; both spec tables are amended with the real macOS limitation. `child_process` was not an option — `08` PSC-8 restricts it, and widening a security rule for a diagnostic is what `CLAUDE.md` §2 forbids |
| 2026-11-07 | audit | `M1-T4` AC, `08` §3.7.10, `02` D13 | **`M1-T4`'s "`sec.crypto.kat` (RFC 5869, **libsodium vectors**)" had no libsodium vector anywhere.** The `secretstream` block asserted three constants and then round-tripped a value through the same implementation — a consistency check, not a known answer: an implementation that got the nonce schedule, the subkey derivation or the Poly1305 tag wrong in the *same* way on both sides would have passed. `shared/testing/secretstream-vector.ts` is a frozen vector now, asserted against `libsodium-wrappers` in `kat.test.ts` and against `sodium-native` in `stream.contract.test.ts` | A **pull** vector, because that is the direction that can be fixed: `crypto_secretstream_xchacha20poly1305_init_push` generates its header randomly and libsodium exposes no way to supply one. Pinning the pull side constrains everything that matters anyway — header-to-subkey derivation, the sequential nonce schedule, the per-chunk tag byte and the authenticator — since all of them must be reproduced exactly for the plaintext to come back. Both implementations opening the **same fixed bytes** is also strictly stronger than the cross-decrypt test beside it, which the audit flagged for comparing no bytes: two implementations agreeing with each other could both be wrong in the same way, and `02` D13 reserves the right to swap one — so what must hold is that the replacement reads the archives the current one wrote. Four non-vacuity cases come with it (wrong key, flipped header bit, chunks out of order, one flipped ciphertext bit). A test caught that coupling the moment the location moved. The deterministic guarantee is now asserted directly: a **failed** temp write leaves the original byte for byte, which is what stops a half-written heartbeat from destroying the lock it was refreshing. And `readLock`'s argument — "a lock file is not evidence of anything unless it can be read" — is sound about a *corrupt* file and was being asked to cover one that was merely *being written*; with the rename it only has to cover the case it was written for, which is now pinned separately |

The curl finding is the kind that hides for a long time, because `--retry 3` *reads* as a retry and the flag that makes it one is separate; a reviewer checking "does it retry?" sees yes. | 2026-11-10 | M1-T19 | `02` §3.3.5, `03` §3.9.3 | **The fairness ledger is posted by `complete`, in the same transaction, crediting the completer.** Three decisions, each with a quiet wrong form: posting it separately could record work for a completion that rolled back; charging every completion rather than only a chore would make a favour look like an obligation met; and crediting the *assignee* would miss exactly the case fairness exists to see — somebody covering for an absent member. Both wrong forms are falsified: crediting the assignee fails "crediting the completer", and charging every completion fails "posts nothing for a completed one-off task". The ledger is also append-only with no update or delete — a correction is a `reversal` entry pointing at what it undoes, because a ledger you can edit is a ledger nobody trusts |

On the mechanism: a side table recording "rows 1–40 were removed lawfully" would be forgeable at leisure, so the claim lives **inside** the chain where every later row covers it. A rewrap replaces the `slots` — that is its purpose, after a recovery-key rotation — so an AD over the whole header makes every rewrapped archive undecryptable. The AD is now the SHA-256 of the canonical JSON of `{format, instanceId, createdAt, dataGen, body}`. | Nothing is lost, and the reasoning is what makes that checkable rather than asserted: every slot is an AEAD wrap of the DEK under a new `AAD.backupDek` (binding instance, creation time, route and key), and every `keyringSnapshot` wrap uses `AAD.keyringSlot` byte-identically to `keyring.json`'s — so a slot swapped in from another archive yields a different DEK and the body fails its own authentication, and a restore can reuse `openSlot` unchanged. The core still binds the body to *this* archive. What an attacker with write access gains is the ability to make an archive they cannot read fail to decrypt, which is vandalism of a file they can already delete. The alternative — rewriting the body on every rotation — is fourteen archives × 5 GB of re-encryption and 70 GB of free space, so a `rewrap` that could not be cheap would not be used |

Two cases now: an encrypted snapshot that verifies (which also asserts the snapshot really is encrypted, or the test would pass against a build writing plaintext backups), and one given the **wrong** key, which must fail — the error a "skip the check when there is no key" workaround would have introduced |
| 2026-11-11 | M1-T27 | `08` §3.7.11 | **The passphrase slot could open the body and not the database, which is a feature that does not work.** §3.7.11 says the keyring snapshot carries `RDK_g` "wrapped by MK and RK **only**" — so a host restoring with a memorised passphrase got a decrypted tar and an unopenable file, while `02` §9.3 requires that "a passphrase-slot restore succeeds when the slot exists". The snapshot now carries whatever slots its generation has. | Found by the test for that criterion, which failed at `rootDataKeyFor`. The line also contradicted §3.7.4's own keyring: `keys add-passphrase` gives it a `kind: 'passphrase'` slot wrapping exactly `RDK_g` under a scrypt-stretched passphrase, so wrapping the root key under a passphrase is already an accepted property of the product rather than a line a backup crosses — which is what settled it, because the alternative reading would have meant the keyring had the weakness and the backup was protecting against it |
| 2026-11-11 | M1-T27 | `02` §3.14.4 | Three implementation clarifications written back into the step list. **Step 8 moves before step 7**: the `restored_from` row goes into the *extracted* database, so a failure writing it aborts with nothing moved rather than leaving a host holding a restored database *and* an error. **Tar entry names are checked before any path is joined**, and the refusal is raised after the stream ends — `next(error)` from `tar-stream`'s entry handler escapes as an *uncaught exception* on a later tick than the `await` meant to catch it, which Vitest reported after the test had already passed. **Step 7's rekey is deferred to `M8-T25`** and the archive's keyring is written only when the host has none. | The traversal one is the interesting failure: the check worked, the file was never written, and the error was unusable — a refusal that arrives as an uncaught exception is a refusal a caller cannot handle, which for a path-traversal guard is the entire point of having one. Caught only because `pnpm verify` reports unhandled errors separately from failures, which is the second time today that reading the gate's *whole* output rather than the lines I expected has mattered |
| 2026-11-11 | method | `CLAUDE.md` §6 | **I wrote "both specs amended" in a commit message before amending them.** §6 requires the spec change in the **same commit** as the code, and `c7d5855` claimed a change that was still only in my head. Amended in the following commit, which is not the same thing. | The claim was true of my intent and false of the commit, and a reader auditing `08` §3.7.11 against `c7d5855` would have found the spec still saying the opposite of the code. The habit: write the spec edit *first*, then the code, then one commit — the order the rule implies and the order that makes the message checkable. Recorded rather than quietly fixed because the failure mode is specifically the one `CLAUDE.md` §8 calls faking evidence, in miniature |
## Deferred follow-ups

Small, *known* improvements that were deliberately not folded into the task that found them, because
they carry their own reasoning or their own blast radius. Not a backlog of wishes: each one is a
defect or a weakness already understood, with the reason for deferring it stated. Tick it with the
commit that closes it, or strike it through with a dated line saying why it stopped mattering.

- **One unreproduced failure in `scheduler.contract.test.ts`** (found `M1-T26`, 2026-11-11). A
  single `pnpm verify` reported `scheduler.contract.test.ts (17 tests | 1 failed)` with no detail
  captured before the run ended. Five subsequent runs — three of that file alone, the whole storage
  suite once and two full `pnpm verify` passes — were green, so it is recorded rather than chased:
  the suite drives lease and fire arithmetic through a `VirtualClock`, and `M1-T23` already found
  that `job_partitions.last_claimed_at` is millisecond-resolution, so a timing dependency under the
  parallelism of a full verify is the shape to suspect. If it recurs, re-run with
  `--reporter=verbose` and capture which case it was before doing anything else.
- **The `eligibleOf` test helper is a cast duplicated in four files.** `calendar.contract.test.ts`,
  `lists.contract.test.ts` and `memory.contract.test.ts` each build an `EligibleLabelSet` with
  `as unknown as never` over every label in the database, and the fourth copy was almost written at
  `M1-T26` before the assertion was changed to a row count instead. The legitimate constructor is
  `computeEligible(labels, audience, snapshot, flow)`, which needs a `ReadEligibility` that `M5-T2`
  builds — so until then the cast is the only option and it should live in the harness, once, with
  the reason. Close it at the M1 exit gate, or at `M5-T2` if the real constructor lands first.
- **`retention.sweep`, `group.purge` and `privacy.erase_person` have no handler registration yet.**
  `M1-T26` builds the three machines and registers their kinds (`retention/jobs.ts`), and the
  `PoolWorker` can claim them — but nothing wires a handler to a machine, because the composition
  root that would do it is `M3-T2`'s. The jobs are therefore enqueueable and not yet runnable
  outside a test. Close it with the server's boot wiring.
- [ ] **`unwrapId` throws an untyped `Error`** (`shared/src/ids.ts`, found `M1-T16`, 2026-11-08).
  Same defect as `internedId`'s, fixed there: the storage code is a message prefix, so
  `exitCodeForStorageCode` and `doctor` cannot see it. Deferred because the fix is not purely
  mechanical — its one call site is `_columns.ts`'s `fromDriver`, where a malformed id comes **out
  of a column**, so the honest code is arguably `STORAGE_CORRUPT` (exit 65, data) rather than the
  `STORAGE_ID_INVALID` (exit 1, bug) it passes today. Changing a column mapper's exit class is a
  decision about what a host sees when a database is damaged, and `timezone.test.ts`'s comment
  already asserts the `STORAGE_CORRUPT` reading. Close it with `M1-T17`, which is the first task to
  read labeled rows through that mapper in anger.
- [x] **The audit chain has no re-anchor, so a retention purge will break it** (`storage/src/repos/audit.ts`,
  found `M1-T21`, 2026-11-11). `08` §895 retains audit events for a year "(hash chain re-anchored on
  purge)" and `verifyChain` now requires a chain to start at `seq` 1 with the genesis `prev_hash` —
  which is what catches `08` T-T02's deletion of the oldest rows, and which a purge therefore trips.
  Deferred because the mechanism is a design decision belonging with the purge that needs it: the
  obvious form does **not** work (rewriting the first survivor's `prev_hash` changes that row's own
  hash, because the hash covers it, so the whole surviving chain would need rehashing), and the
  alternative is a stored purged-up-to `(seq, hash)` per chain that `verifyChain` accepts as a
  continuation — which is a second place the chain's truth lives and wants its own integrity story.
  Until then a purged chain reads as broken, which is the safe direction and is pinned by a test.
  Close it with `M1-T26` (retention & deletion).
  **Closed at `M1-T26`** (`07c4a50`, tests in `retention/audit-reanchor.int.test.ts`). Neither form
  above was taken: the purged-up-to `(seq, hash)` lives **inside** the chain, as an
  `audit.reanchor` event appended at the head before any row is removed, so the claim "these rows
  were removed lawfully" is covered by every row written after it rather than needing an integrity
  story of its own. Two things the deferral did not foresee, both found by mutation. The re-anchor
  must be **deleted** by a later purge that reaches past it, or it is left stranded above a gap it
  does not vouch for and two lawful purges produce a chain that reads as tampered with. And the
  "keep one row so there is something to anchor" guard the first draft had is unnecessary *and*
  wrong — the re-anchor's own `prev_hash` is the last removed row's hash, so it vouches for itself,
  and the guard kept a row the host's configured ceiling says to remove.
- [ ] **Labeled reads whose only I1 test passes an empty eligible set** (`M1-T17`–`M1-T20`'s
  contract tests, found `M1-T21`, 2026-11-11). The empty set is refused by an early return, so such
  a test passes identically against a reader with no `label_id` filter at all — measured on the two
  trace reads, where deleting the filter left the suite green. `M1-T20`'s and `M1-T21`'s tests now
  also pass a **non-empty** set that excludes the row's label; the earlier tasks' do not, and the
  readers themselves are correct. Deferred rather than swept because it is a one-line addition per
  read across four files and ~40 reads, and doing it as its own pass means each one can be falsified
  by deleting the filter it covers. Close it with the `M1` exit-gate review.

A rename does not rewrite history (ADRs, STATUS, changeset entries), and the drill asserts the rename actually happened — a drill that passed because the codemod did nothing would be green and useless. **Measured on a scratch clone:** rename exit 0 (176 files, 0 skipped), `pnpm install` 0, `pnpm verify` 0 with 1044 tests, `pnpm --filter @zzbrand/docs build` 0, and an **independent** leftover scan — not the tool's own report — finding nothing outside the allowlist. Two required checks had to be made event-stable first, and `zizmor`'s workflow-level path filter had to come off, or the queue would have stalled on a check that never runs. The merge queue itself needs an organisation-owned repo (**H-6**); its `11` §3.8.3 settings are documented ready. `docs/architecture/ci.md` records all of it — `6a657b4`, `5da3303`, `aa95b34`, `907334b`

#### Execution note

**Written 2026-11-01.** Order is `14` §2's table order. Deviations and their reasons are below.

**Spikes.** None for M0 (`14` §2). The §0.7 consistency pass ran as `M0-T23` and came back clean —
504 canonical tasks across 16 milestones, 552 spec tasks in `02`–`13` §10, **0 unmapped spec tasks,
0 unknown references**, and `STATUS.md` in exact agreement with every task table. Four real
inconsistencies were found and fixed (see *Plan changes*).

**Owner checkpoints.** `D-2` was pulled forward to M0 by the owner, so it was decided here rather
than at M7/M8 — see DL-8 and [ADR-024](../adr/ADR-024-product-name.md). `D-3`, `D-5`, `D-7` and
`D-8` were also decided now, since each was either already determined by the facts on the ground or
costs nothing to settle early (DL-1 to DL-4). `D-1` and `D-6` wait for M9, where there will be
measured cost data to decide with. `D-4` is the money part and is the owner ask H-1.

**Task order, with the deviations.**

1. `M0-T20` (ADRs) and `M0-T23` (the plan consistency pass) ran **first**, in parallel sub-agents,
   because both are `‖`, neither depends on the scaffold, and `M0-T23`'s checker is the thing that
   tells me whether the plan I am about to execute is self-consistent. It was worth knowing that
   before writing code, and it found four defects.
2. `M0-T1` (scaffold) next, on the main thread.
3. Then `M0-T2` → `M0-T19` in table order. `M0-T5` (`BRAND`) was partly pulled into `M0-T1`,
   because every one of the 24 `package.json` files needs the final npm scope and there is no
   sensible way to scaffold without it. `M0-T5` is ticked only when its full acceptance criteria
   are met (the `13` §4.1 shape, the exclusions, and the planted-violation fixture).
4. `M0-T21` (branch protection) last, since it needs `ci-ok` to exist.

**Sub-agents.** `M0-T20` and `M0-T23` went to sub-agents (disjoint file sets, no commits from the
agent, the main thread verifies and commits). Four more sub-agents ran the D-2 clearance research
and one re-verified the entire toolchain against the live registries. Everything touching
`packages/core` and every migration stays serial, per `14` §0.4 — and `M0-T23` found three M4 rows
mis-tagged `‖` over exactly those paths, now corrected.

**Risks taken into M0, and what happened.**

- *The plan's third-party facts were a day old.* Re-verification found five that had moved, two of
  which would have broken the M0 gate: `typescript-eslint` cannot run on TypeScript 7, and
  `vitest.workspace.ts` is removed in Vitest 5 and now fails **silently**. Recorded as spine
  amendment **A31** plus [ADR-025](../adr/ADR-025-typescript-version-pin.md).
- *Naming was the real risk.* Every name in `13` §3.A.3's shortlist failed, and two replacements
  were chosen and reversed. The `BRAND` + codemod + weekly-drill machinery is what made that cost
  an hour instead of a week; it earned its place before a line of product code existed.
- *Guards that report success while enforcing nothing.* The first dependency-cruiser config excluded
  `/dist/`, and since workspace imports resolve through `node_modules` into `dist/`, every
  cross-package edge was invisible. It printed "no violations" over an empty graph. Found by
  planting a violation, not by reading. This is the argument for `M0-T4`'s
  planted-violation-fixture requirement, and it generalises: **a guard is not done until it has
  been seen to fail.**

**Needed from the human.** H-1 (API budget) through H-7, listed above. None of them block M0: the
scaffold, the guards, CI and the docs skeleton need no credentials. H-6 (securing the `comvivo`
names) is the one with a clock on it, because the repository is public.
